Now sourced directly from your uploaded daily FII/DII data — no more search gaps. A genuinely mixed week: FII flipped between buying and selling day to day with no clear trend, while DII was a strong, consistent buyer, most emphatically on Wednesday (+₹5,842 Cr) and Thursday (+₹4,353 Cr).

Reading the week
Despite the India Stocks & Indices tab showing a broad equity pullback this week (Nifty -0.83%, breadth turning negative), both FII and DII were net buyers for the week overall — FII modestly (+₹1,228 Cr) and DII substantially (+₹9,286 Cr, the strongest single-week DII buying in this report's four-week history). This is a useful corrective to the earlier read built on partial data: the pullback wasn't driven by institutional selling: it happened despite net institutional buying, pointing to a broader risk-off move (likely the crude oil rebound flagged in the FX and Commodities sections above) rather than a flows-driven story.

Derivatives Data — FII Positioning

A notable shift this week: FII index-futures positioning flipped to net long mid-week (11-12 Aug, ~459-460K contracts long) before commentary on 13 Aug described a net short stance (~168,702 contracts) — these two readings are inconsistent with each other and likely reflect different data conventions between sources, so treat both with caution.

11-12 Aug reading
~+459-460K net long
described as "bullish stance" by source
13 Aug reading
~-168,702 net short
described as "confident bearish stance" by a different source
13 Aug Options
Calls & Puts both net short
+17,569 long calls vs +75,471 short; +32,240 long puts vs +57,861 short
Data confidence note
The two source readings for this week directly contradict each other on direction (net long vs net short) — rather than pick one, both are shown so you can judge which source you trust more, or verify directly via NSE's participant-wise OI report. The options-side reading (net short both calls and puts) is more consistent across sources and reads as protective hedging stacked on modest cash selling, similar to recent weeks.

Rupee context
The rupee gave back last week's gains as Brent crude rebounded (+5% Monday alone) on renewed doubts over a US-Iran Strait-of-Hormuz agreement — the same dynamic in reverse from two weeks ago, when a crude collapse strengthened the rupee. RBI intervention (state-run banks selling dollars) was again reported as cushioning the move. Higher US Treasury yields (10Y near 4.70%, also oil-driven) added external pressure on the dollar side too.

06

Executive Summary — Where Is Institutional Money Flowing?

The big picture
The three-week recovery paused this week, and the trigger looks like the same one that drove it — just in reverse. Nifty 50 fell 0.83%, breadth flipped from strongly positive (959 Adv/595 Dec) to negative (635 Adv/931 Dec), and DMA-based breadth pulled back across every cap bucket for the first time in a month. The common thread across FX, equities, and (likely) debt this week is Brent crude's rebound — the same Iran/Strait-of-Hormuz diplomatic uncertainty that collapsed oil prices two weeks ago (helping the rally) reversed course this week (Brent +5% on Monday alone), pressuring the rupee and, most likely, Indian bond yields.

Institutional cash flows were positive on both sides this week — which makes the equity pullback more notable, not less: FII net bought a modest +₹1,228 Cr and DII net bought a strong +₹9,286 Cr (the biggest single week for DII buying in this report's four-week history), yet Nifty 50 still fell 0.83% and breadth turned decisively negative. That combination — net institutional buying alongside a broad price decline — points toward the equity weakness being driven by something other than flows: most likely the crude oil rebound flagged throughout this report, hitting valuations and sentiment even as domestic and (net) foreign money kept coming in. The derivatives picture remains murkier (Section 2) — two sources disagree on whether FIIs are net long or net short index futures — worth treating with real caution rather than picking a side.

DMA breadth confirms this is a broad pullback, not a narrow one: every cap bucket's % above both DMA50 and DMA200 declined this week (see the Market Breadth section in the India Stocks & Indices tab), and crossovers flipped negative — 94 stocks dropped below DMA200 against only 39 crossing above (net -55), and 197 dropped below DMA50 against 92 crossing above (net -105). This mirrors the 3-week improving trend documented in prior editions, now reversing in the same broad-based way it built.

Oil is now the single variable to watch most closely — more so than FII flows or even RBI policy in the near term. Two consecutive weeks have now shown this report's entire macro picture (FX, equities, likely bonds) swinging in lockstep with Brent crude on Iran/Hormuz headline risk. Until that geopolitical situation resolves one way or the other, expect this pattern to keep dominating week-to-week moves.

What to watch next week
Whether the oil-driven pullback extends or the market finds its footing; whether FII derivatives positioning clarifies (the contradictory readings this week need resolving); and whether DII buying continues to more than offset FII activity as it has in every week this report has covered.

Global ETF Heatmap — Weekly Analysis

Executive Summary — Weekly Change

The big picture
Asia-Pacific led, Latin America lagged, and the split was almost exactly even overall — 20 of 40 country/region ETFs positive, 20 negative. South Korea (+8.22%) was the standout by a wide margin, more than double the next-best performer (Taiwan, +3.86%), both continuing the Asia-Pacific tech/semiconductor strength visible in the US Stocks tab. Colombia (+3.74%), the Netherlands (+3.47%) and Norway (+3.15%) rounded out the top 5. On the downside, Brazil (-3.99%), Mexico (-3.24%) and South Africa (-3.04%) were the weakest — a broad emerging-markets-ex-Asia softness, distinct from the Asia-Pacific strength.
